Doctorat ParisTech THESE [SOURCE: Page_2]

Pour obtenir le grade de docteur délivré par [SOURCE: Page_2]

L’Institut des Sciences et Industries du Vivant et de l’Environnement (AgroParisTech) Spécialité : écotoxicologie [SOURCE: Page_2]

présentée et soutenue publiquement par Sylvain CORBEL le 14 novembre 2014 [SOURCE: Page_2]

Caractérisation des mécanismes de contamination de la tomate Solanum lycopersicum var. MicroTom par des cyanotoxines de type microcystine sur un système sol-plante et risques sanitaires associés [SOURCE: Page_2]

Jury : [SOURCE: Page_2]

Claire Chenu, Professeur, AgroParisTech (Présidente) Vitor Vasconcelos, Professeur, Université de Porto (Rapporteur) Muriel Raveton, Maître de Conférences, Université Joseph Fourier-Grenoble (Rapporteur) Nathatlie Demont-Caulet, Maître de Conférences, Université Paris Diderot (Examinatrice) Noureddine Bouaïcha, Maître de Conférences, Université Paris-Sud (Co-directeur de thèse) Christian Mougin, Directeur de Recherche, INRA Versailles (Co-directeur de thèse) [SOURCE: Page_2]

Remerciements [SOURCE: Page_4]

Remerciements Je remercie sincèrement Muriel Raveton et Vitor Vascancelos, rapporteurs de cette étude, ainsi que Claire Chenu et Nathalie Demont-Caulet, pour avoir accepté d’évaluer ce travail. [SOURCE: Page_4]

Ce projet, s’inscrivant dans un Domaine d’Intérêt Majeur (DIM) Agrosciences, Ecologie des Territoires et Alimentation (ASTREA), a été financé par la Région Ile-de-France. J’en profite donc pour remercier toutes les personnes qui animent activement ce DIM notamment au travers des journées des doctorants et post-doctorants. Je pense tout particulièrement à Palmira de Carvalho, Sabine Riou et Christine Aubry. [SOURCE: Page_4]

J’en profite aussi pour remercier l’unité Physico-chimie et Ecotoxicologie des SolS d’Agrosystèmes Contaminés (PESSAC) et le laboratoire d’Ecologie Systématique et Evolution (ESE) pour m’avoir accueilli et avoir permis de réaliser l’ensemble des expérimentations tout au long de ces trois années. [SOURCE: Page_4]

Ce travail est aussi le résultat d’un suivi indéniable de la part de Christian Mougin et Noureddine Bouaïcha. Beaucoup de discussions et de réunions ont été nécessaires pour pouvoir rendre ce projet fertile dans une unité, PESSAC, où la plante était peu présente. Sans oublier ces cyanobactéries et leur cortège de toxines, également toutes nouvelles à PESSAC. Mais, en parlant de fertilité, rassurez-vous les plants de tomates ont su dépasser la phase de reproduction et donner de jolies petites tomates … [SOURCE: Page_4]

Avec toutes ces « nouveautés » dans un projet qui tout compte fait s’est déroulé très rapidement, il m’est apparu indispensable de m’entourer de plusieurs scientifiques disposant de compétances très diverses, notamment pour la composition de mon comité de pilotage. Je pense notamment à François Laurent, pour tout ce qui conernait les plantes, Fabrice Martin- Laurent, pour la microbiologie des sols. Je n’oublie pas non plus la gente féminine avec Marie-Chantal Canivenc, plus spécialisée dans l’expérimentation animale. On n’aura pas été jusque-là mais c’est avec grand plaisir que j’ai pu échanger avec toi. [SOURCE: Page_4]

Je pense également à tous les autres collègues de PESSAC et de BIOGER qui ont su me guider au mieux au cours de ces trois années : Olivier Crouzet, Folkert van Oort, Isabelle Lamy et Jean-Marc Pradier notamment au travers des choix que j’ai dû effectuer. J’ai aussi une pensée toute particulière pour l’équipe de chimie de l’unité. Si cela n’était pas forcement prévu initialement Sylvie et Ghislaine ont su m’accueillir, me guider, m’expliquer quelques rudiments de chimie, au novice que j’étais. Merci à vous tous. [SOURCE: Page_4]

III [SOURCE: Page_4]

Remerciements [SOURCE: Page_5]

Je tiens également à remercier l’école doctorale ABIES qui propose des formations indispensables aux jeunes doctorants. Notamment les personnes en charge de l’organisation de ces formations, Irina Vassileva et Aline Gheysens ainsi qu’aux formateurs et en particulier Donald White et Eric Birlouez, dévoués et débordants de motivation. J’ai en particulier bénéficié énormément d’aide de la part de Donald. La langue de Shakespeare est loin d’être « ma tasse de thea », j’ai beaucoup appris en sa présence et j’espère rencontrer des formateurs tout aussi compétents pour combler mes lacunes. Quant aux futurs doctorants d’ABIES, j’espère que malgré le départ en retraite de Donald, ils auront la chance de côtoyer un formateur de sa trempe … . [SOURCE: Page_5]

Vous avez été nombreux à m’aider dans mes différentes expérimentations que ce soit au travers des conseils, de l’aide continue pendant ces (trop) longues manipulations mais aussi en vous dévouant pour m’aider lors des fins de manipulations, où les journées n’étaient plus assez longues. Je pense notamment à Christelle Marrauld, Virginie Grondin, Ghislaine Delarue, Jessica Le Golvan, Françoise Poiroux, Nathalie Cheviron, Amélie Trouvé, Jean- Pierre de l’unité Pessac. Je n’oublie pas non plus l’aide de Jean-Pascal Meunier et Lilian Dahuron de l’Institut Jean-Pierre Bougin sur le site de l’INRA de Versailles qui nous ont permis de cultiver les tomates en toutes circonstances. Et il y a aussi les personnes très accueillantes de l’INRA de Dijon, Agroécologie notamment Jérémie Beguet et David Bru qui ont collaboré à ce travail. Enfin je tenais à remercier Valérie Bergheaud et Valérie Dumeny, de l’unité Environnement et Grandes Cultures (EGC) de l’INRA de Grignon pour leur aide, ainsi que tous ceux qui ont pu, à un moment, ou à un autre, participer à ces travaux et que j’aurai malencontreusement oubliés. [SOURCE: Page_5]

Enfin, merci pour le soutien et la bonne humeur de l’ensemble des collègues du bureau, Benjamin, Elodie, Léa, Kévin, Thérèse, Marine, Francesca, Gaël, Muhamad … . [SOURCE: Page_5]

J’en profite également pour remercier tous mes amis et collègues « galériens » de thèse issus de Besançon, François et Rémi. Il est tellement réconfortant de pouvoir se dire : « je ne suis pas le seul dans ce cas », à la seule différence que je devais être le seul à avoir soutenu ne pas vouloir faire de thèse en master 2 et maintenant la Soutenance se rapproche… . A ce sujet, je tiens à remercier Badr Allaoui-Sossé et Mohamed, encadrants de stage de mon master 2, sans qui « je ne me serais sûrement pas lancé dans ce long projet ». [SOURCE: Page_5]

Remerciements [SOURCE: Page_6]

Enfin et par ce que ce sont sûrement vous qui avez le plus souvent enduré mon caractère morose et mes nombreux moments de doutes, je tiens à remercier tout particulièrement mes proches et en particulier Adeline, ma femme, toujours présente et à l’écoute. Et aussi Merci à mes parents et grands-parents qui m’avez porté et soutenu tout au long de mes études, et qui malgré tout avez essayé « de raccrocher les wagons » lorsque j’abordais mon sujet de thèse …. [SOURCE: Page_6]

Glossaire des abréviations BMAA : L-beta-N-methylamino-L-alanine (neurotoxine) CYN : cylindrospermopsine (cytotoxine) DW : dry weight (poids sec) i.p. : injection intra-péritonéal LD50 : dose nécessaire pour induire la mort de 50% des individus MC(s) : microcystine(s) dont les congénères cités sont répertoriés ci-dessous selon les acides aminés: positions    MC-LR        MC-RR     MC-LY       MC-LW         MC-LF        MC-YR X         leucine    arginine   leucine      leucine      leucine      tyrosine Z        arginine    arginine   tyrosine   tryptophane phénylalanine   arginine [SOURCE: Page_12]

Liste des figures Figure 1: Photographie d'une efflorescence de cyanobactéries (Microcystis aeruginosa) dans le barrage Cheffia (Algérie) en 2011 (N. Bouaïcha).............................. 3 Figure 2: Schéma résumant la structuration du manuscrit. ......................................... 6 Figure 3: Structures of several neurotoxins, (A) anatoxin-a, (B) homoanatoxin, (C) anatoxin-a(s), (D) saxitoxin and (E) amino acid L-beta-N-methylamino-L- alanine (BMAA). ........................................................................................................... 16 Figure 4: Structures of different hepatotoxins, (A) microcystin where X and Z are variable L-amino acids, (B) nodularin and (C) cylindrospermopsin. ......................... 18 Figure 5: Structure chimique générale des microcystines constituées de 7 acides aminés dont X et Z sont les 2 acides aminés variables. ................................................... 45 Figure 6: (A) Localisation sur une photographie aérienne (Géoportail) du site expérimental de la Pierre-Plate (Versailles, France) sous prairie permanente, délimité par le polygone bleu. Ce dernier peut être divisé en deux parties distinctes selon les altitudes visibles sur le profil altimétrique (trait rouge) (B) entre les deux points. Le sol utilisé provenait de la partie « haute » du site expérimental de la Pierre-Plate. .......... 46 Figure 7: Schéma représentant le cycle phénologique simplifié de la tomate Solanum lycopersicum variété MicroTom. ..................................................................... 48 Figure 8: Schéma du dispositif permettant la culture des cyanobactéries de la souche Microcystis aeruginosa (PCC7820) dans un milieu contenant du bicarbonate de sodium marqué au carbone 14. La pompe 1 permettait l’apport d’air exogène, stérilisé par filtration, dans le milieu de culture par barbotage permanent. La pompe 2 permettait de récupérer une partie de l’air interne à la culture pouvant contenir du carbone 14 minéralisé (14C-CO$_2$). Le circuit rouge permettait de mélanger l’air exogène à l’air interne à la culture. Le circuit vert quant-à-lui permettait de capturer une partie de l’air ambiant de la culture mis à buller dans un piège à CO 2 avant d’être aspiré par un système de ventilation. .............................................................................. 55 Figure 9: Schéma du montage expérimental permettant de suivre la minéralisation de la microcystine-LR marquée au carbone 14 apportée par irrigation sur la colonne de sol. ..................................................................................................... 65 Figure 10: Chromatogramme obtenu par chromatographie liquide (A238) après injection d’un extrait aqueux de cyanobactéries (75% MeOH, v/v) obtenu après culture de la souche Microcystis aeruginosa (PCC7820) où apparaissent les 4 principaux congénères de microcystines : MC-LR , MC-LY, MC-LW et MC-LF. ......... 81 Figure 11: Chromatogrammes obtenu en UPLC-MS/MS (MRM) après injection d’un extrait de cyanobactéries (75% MeOH, v/v) obtenu après culture de la souche Microcystis aeruginosa (PCC7820) où apparaissent les 13 congénères différents identifiés........................................................................................................................ 84 Figure 12: Taux de germination (%) des tomates Solanum lycopersicum variétés MicroTom (A) et Saint-Pierre (B) et de la laitue Lactuca sativa variété capitata (C) après 7 jours d’exposition à différentes concentrations (50 µg L-1 à 1 g L-1) de chlorure de cadmium (noir), ou à l’extrait de Microcystis aeruginosa (rouge) contenant différentes concentrations en microcystines (50 µg L-1 à 20 mg L-1). XVII [SOURCE: Page_18]

XVIII [SOURCE: Page_19]

Introduction Générale [SOURCE: Page_20]

Introduction Générale [SOURCE: Page_20]

Contexte [SOURCE: Page_20]

Ces dernières décennies, les changements sociétaux des pays développés n’ont cessé [SOURCE: Page_20]

d’accroitre la pression anthropique sur l’environnement avec notamment l’industrialisation et [SOURCE: Page_20]

la modernisation de l’agriculture. Celle-ci s’est vue dotée de nouveaux engins mais a aussi [SOURCE: Page_20]

utilisé de façon croissante des intrants : produits phytosanitaires et fertilisants. En [SOURCE: Page_20]

conséquence, l’exploitation des gisements de minerais nécessaires à certaines activités n’a [SOURCE: Page_20]

cessé d’augmenter. C’est le cas du phosphore et de l’azote dont les quantités extraites étaient [SOURCE: Page_20]

respectivement de 191 Mt en 2005 et 187 Mt en 2011 (Galloway et al., 2008; Jasinski, 2012). [SOURCE: Page_20]

Concernant l’azote, environ la moitié de la production est utilisée pour la fabrication des [SOURCE: Page_20]

engrais industriels épandus sur les surfaces agricoles (Galloway et al., 2008). Ces éléments [SOURCE: Page_20]

essentiels à la croissance des plantes sont apportés à la surface des sols par épandage de [SOURCE: Page_20]

fertilisants industriels, de produits résiduaires ou encore par dépôts atmosphériques en lien [SOURCE: Page_20]

avec les activités humaines (Sharpley et al., 1994; Howarth et al., 1996; Novotny, 2003) [SOURCE: Page_20]

pouvant modifier les cycles biogéochimiques (Russell et al., 2005). Cette forte quantité [SOURCE: Page_20]

d’intrants apportée aux sols entraine une saturation de ces derniers en éléments nutritifs. [SOURCE: Page_20]

L’azote et le phosphore peuvent alors être exportés en grande quantité vers les eaux de surface [SOURCE: Page_20]

ou souterraines, à la suite de phénomènes d’érosion ou de lixiviation (Aber et al., 1989; [SOURCE: Page_20]

Vitousek et al., 1997; Caraco and Cole, 1999). [SOURCE: Page_20]

Les zones telles que les lacs, rivières, estuaires et côtes marines reçoivent des eaux riches [SOURCE: Page_20]

en nutriments azotés et phosphorés. Déjà au début du XXème siècle, suite à l’enrichissement en [SOURCE: Page_20]

éléments nutritifs des milieux aquatiques, il avait été démontré une augmentation de l’activité [SOURCE: Page_20]

biologique dans des tourbières (Weber, 1907) et en mer du Nord (Johnstone, 1908). Ce [SOURCE: Page_20]

1 [SOURCE: Page_20]

Introduction Générale [SOURCE: Page_21]

phénomène d’enrichissement en éléments nutritifs dans les lacs, suivi d’une forte activité [SOURCE: Page_21]

biologique était déjà décrit par Rodhe (1969), durant les temps géologiques et se définissait [SOURCE: Page_21]

comme une eutrophisation des milieux aquatiques. Cependant, l’utilisation importante [SOURCE: Page_21]

d’intrants à forte valeur nutritive accélère ce phénomène et semble d’autant plus accentué par [SOURCE: Page_21]

le réchauffement climatique actuel (Ficke et al., 2007). En conséquent, l’intensification de [SOURCE: Page_21]

l’activité biologique et principalement des organismes producteurs primaires va induire des [SOURCE: Page_21]

changements importants de la composition de la structure des réseaux trophiques (Smith et al., [SOURCE: Page_21]

2006). [SOURCE: Page_21]

Les cyanobactéries sont capables de proliférer très rapidement sur une courte période [SOURCE: Page_21]

(Scott and Marcarelli, 2012), leur biomasse devient alors importante et une écume verte ou [SOURCE: Page_21]

efflorescence ou « bloom » de cyanobactéries se développe à la surface de l’eau (Figure 1). [SOURCE: Page_21]

Cette prolifération intervient au détriment des autres espèces (plantes, animaux et [SOURCE: Page_21]

microorganismes) présentes dans le milieu. De plus, certaines souches présentes dans les [SOURCE: Page_21]

milieux aquatiques sont capables de synthétiser des métabolites secondaires toxiques pour un [SOURCE: Page_21]

certain nombre d’espèces. Le rôle des toxines produites par les cyanobactéries reste encore [SOURCE: Page_21]

mal compris mais elles les rendraient plus compétitives dans l’environnement. Ces toxines [SOURCE: Page_21]

sont également capables de perturber le métabolisme d’organismes non inféodés au milieu [SOURCE: Page_21]

aquatique et notamment les mammifères terrestres et l’homme. Elles sont souvent classées [SOURCE: Page_21]

selon les organes ciblés par les toxines : hépatotoxines (le foie), neurotoxines (le système [SOURCE: Page_21]

nerveux) et dermatotoxines (la peau et les muqueuses). Les toxines ont également des [SOURCE: Page_21]

propriétés cytotoxiques qui peuvent induire la production cellulaire d’espèces réactives de [SOURCE: Page_21]

l’oxygène (Pflugmacher, 2004). [SOURCE: Page_21]

2 [SOURCE: Page_21]

Introduction Générale [SOURCE: Page_22]

Figure 1: Photographie d'une efflorescence de cyanobactéries (Microcystis aeruginosa) dans le barrage Cheffia (Algérie) en 2011 (N. Bouaïcha). [SOURCE: Page_22]

Ces toxines représentent donc un véritable problème pour les ressources en eau [SOURCE: Page_22]

puisqu’elles sont présentes dans des eaux destinées à usage domestique (eaux potables), à des [SOURCE: Page_22]

fins récréatives ou encore dans les eaux d’irrigation. L’agriculture est une activité très [SOURCE: Page_22]

consommatrice d’eau puisée dans des retenues d’eau naturelles ou artificielles, peu ou pas [SOURCE: Page_22]

contrôlées. La forte persistance de ces toxines et en particulier des microcystines, dans le [SOURCE: Page_22]

milieu aquatique (plusieurs semaines) (Jones and Orr, 1994; Edwards et al., 2008) peut alors [SOURCE: Page_22]

les rendre dangereuses lors d’une irrigation répétée des sols et des cultures. [SOURCE: Page_22]

3 [SOURCE: Page_22]

Introduction Générale [SOURCE: Page_23]

Objectifs de mes travaux de recherche et structuration du mémoire Le devenir et l’impact de cyanotoxines apportées par irrigation dans des agrosystèmes [SOURCE: Page_23]

restent peu étudiés comme nous le montrons dans le travail bibliographique présenté [SOURCE: Page_23]

(Chapitre 1). De l’apport aux sols agricoles de cyanotoxines découlent trois principaux [SOURCE: Page_23]

risques : [SOURCE: Page_23]

 Un risque de détérioration de la ressource sol avec une possible contamination avec des [SOURCE: Page_23]

toxines dont on sait peu de chose sur leur devenir dans les sols. Le sol est bien sûr un [SOURCE: Page_23]

support de production mais c’est aussi une niche écologique abritant de nombreux [SOURCE: Page_23]

organismes : microorganismes, mésofaune, macrofaune…, qui jouent un rôle essentiel [SOURCE: Page_23]

dans la structuration, le fonctionnement et in fine la fertilité du sol. Ainsi, il faut [SOURCE: Page_23]

comprendre les perturbations que peuvent causer les toxines de cyanobactéries. [SOURCE: Page_23]

 Un risque de baisse des rendements agricoles après irrigation avec des eaux [SOURCE: Page_23]

contaminées en cyanotoxines peut être envisagé. Une baisse de production présenterait [SOURCE: Page_23]

alors un risque économique pour les agriculteurs utilisant ces ressources en eau [SOURCE: Page_23]

contaminées. [SOURCE: Page_23]

 Enfin le risque de transfert des toxines du sol irrigué vers les plantes qui y sont [SOURCE: Page_23]

cultivées, reste possible. A la fois le devenir des toxines dans le sol agricole mais aussi [SOURCE: Page_23]

le transfert et l’accumulation de toxines dans la plante doivent être mieux connus. Ces [SOURCE: Page_23]

deux derniers points nous amènent à quantifier un possible transfert dans les parties [SOURCE: Page_23]

comestibles des plantes et à déterminer si un risque sanitaire résulte de cette [SOURCE: Page_23]

accumulation. [SOURCE: Page_23]

4 [SOURCE: Page_23]

Introduction Générale [SOURCE: Page_24]

Dans ce contexte, les principaux objectifs de mes recherches sont de répondre aux [SOURCE: Page_24]

questions suivantes, lesquelles seront reprises dans chaque chapitre : [SOURCE: Page_24]

Question 1 : Quels sont les effets toxicologiques de l’extrait toxique de la souche Microcystis aeruginosa (PCC7820) contenant différentes microcystines, testé in vitro ? [SOURCE: Page_24]

Hypothèse 1 : L’extrait de cyanobactéries présente des propriétés toxiques importantes pour les plantes et les microorganismes, liées à sa composition en toxines. [SOURCE: Page_24]

Q 1.1 : Quels sont les différents congénères de microcystines présents dans l’extrait obtenu à partir de notre culture de la souche Microcystis aeruginosa (PCC7820) ? Q 1.2 : Quels sont les effets d’une exposition in vitro à cet extrait, sur plusieurs variétés de semences ? Q 1.3 : Quels sont les effets toxiques de cet extrait ajouté à l’eau d’irrigation lors d’une exposition sub-chronique, sur les microorganismes des sols et sur les plantules ? [SOURCE: Page_24]

Question 2 : Quels sont les effets de l’exposition sub-chronique ou chronique d’un système sol-plante à un extrait de cyanobactéries ? [SOURCE: Page_24]

Hypothèse 2 : L’utilisation d’une eau contaminée avec un extrait de cyanobactéries modifie (i) la croissance des tomates, Solanum lycopersicum var. MicroTom et (ii) les activités microbiennes du sol. [SOURCE: Page_24]

Q 2.1 : Quels sont les effets d’une irrigation chronique, à long terme, avec des microcystines sur les plantules de tomates ? (Y-a-t-il des modifications du développement ou des paramètres physiologiques ?) Q 2.2 : Quels sont les effets d’une irrigation chronique, à long terme, avec des microcystines sur les microorganismes du sol ? [SOURCE: Page_24]

Question 3 : Que deviennent les microcystines, apportées à un sol agricole ? [SOURCE: Page_24]

Hypothèse 3 : (i) Une partie des toxines va être dégradée par les microorganismes du sol, et une autre partie (ii) va être absorbée par les plants de tomates alors (iii) qu’une partie faiblement bio-disponible restera adsorbée au sol. [SOURCE: Page_24]

Q 3.1 : Les communautés microbiennes du sol sont-elles capables de dégrader les microcystines apportées au sol par irrigation ? [SOURCE: Page_24]

5 [SOURCE: Page_24]

Introduction Générale [SOURCE: Page_25]

Q 3.2 : Les microcystines apportées par irrigation s’adsorbent-elles au niveau du sol ? Sont-elles sujettes à rester biodisponibles dans les sols ? Q 3.3 : Les microcystines sont-elles absorbées par les tomates ? Que deviennent-elles dans la plante ? [SOURCE: Page_25]

Au travers ce manuscrit, nous nous proposons de répondre à ces différents objectifs en structurant le travail comme indiqué dans le schéma ci-dessous (Figure 2). [SOURCE: Page_25]

Chapitre 3  Effets sur les activités                                          Persistance biologiques des sols             Chapitre 5 dans le sol  Effets sur les plantules  Effets sur les activités des de tomates sols  Effets sur les plants de tomates [SOURCE: Page_25]

système sol-plante [SOURCE: Page_25]

Devenir 14C-MC-LR Chapitre 4                                                       SOL PLANTE Figure 2: Schéma résumant la structuration du manuscrit. [SOURCE: Page_25]

Après l’étude bibliographique constituant le chapitre 1, ce mémoire s’organise en plusieurs chapitres schématisés sur la Figure 2, et détaillés ci-dessus : [SOURCE: Page_25]

 Le chapitre 2 décrit l’ensemble des matériels et des méthodes utilisés au cours des travaux.  Le chapitre 3 présente les expériences préliminaires qui permettent de caractériser l’extrait de cyanobactéries obtenu. Cet extrait sera ensuite utilisé pour la totalité des expérimentations sans radioactivité. Dans un second temps, des tests écotoxicologiques sur les différents objects d’études (sol et plante) ont été réalisés afin de caractériser la phytotoxicité et l’écotoxicité de cet extrait. Ces tests ont été réalisés en s’appuyant sur des normes (test de germination, test de luminescence de V. fisheri et essai d’inhibition de la croissance des végétaux par une substance) et 6 [SOURCE: Page_25]

Introduction Générale [SOURCE: Page_26]

sur d’autres indicateurs utilisés en écotoxicologie des sols (activités enzymatiques et nitrification potentielle) sur de courtes périodes d’exposition (7 et 14 jours).  Le chapitre 4 permet de mieux comprendre le comportement dynamique des toxines radiomarquées (14C-MC-LR) apportées par irrigation dans le sol. La capacité des toxines à s’adsorber au sol agricole est obtenue après différents temps d’incubation. La biodisponibilité des toxines dans le sol et leur capacité à être bio- dégradées par les microorganismes ont été mesurées. Enfin, le transfert et l’accumulation de la MC-LR dans des plantes ont pu être estimés.  Enfin, le chapitre 5 permet une étude complète de l’exposition chronique d’un système sol-plante pendant toute la durée du cycle de vie de la plante. Des paramètres liés à la physiologie et à la croissance de la plante ont pu être mesurés pendant    l’exposition   ainsi   qu’à       la   fin.   En   parallèle,   les   paramètres écotoxicologiques du sol ont été pris en considération ainsi que l’accumulation dans les différents organes. [SOURCE: Page_26]

Chapitre 1 : Etude Bibliographique Cyanobacterial toxins: Modes of actions, fate in aquatic and soil ecosystems, phytotoxicity and bioaccumulation in agricultural crops – Revue bibliographique [SOURCE: Page_28]

9 [SOURCE: Page_28]

10 [SOURCE: Page_29]

Etude Bibliographique [SOURCE: Page_30]

Résumé [SOURCE: Page_30]

La fréquence d’efflorescences (blooms) de cyanobactéries est très souvent accompagnée [SOURCE: Page_30]

par la synthèse de cyanotoxines. Les toxines sont classées selon les organes touchés chez [SOURCE: Page_30]

l’homme et les animaux : les hépatotoxines (le foie), les neurotoxines (le système nerveux), [SOURCE: Page_30]

les alcaloïdes cytotoxiques et les dermatotoxines (le derme). Ces toxines ont souvent des [SOURCE: Page_30]

effets secondaires très importants. Lorsque les toxines sont introduites dans des sols cultivés, [SOURCE: Page_30]

par arrosage, elles peuvent modifier les mêmes voies métaboliques chez les plantes que celles [SOURCE: Page_30]

identifiées pour les organes, tissus, cellules ou biolmolécules animales. De plus, plusieurs [SOURCE: Page_30]

indications nous montrent que les plantes cultivées, à des fins d’alimentation, peuvent [SOURCE: Page_30]

accumuler des toxines, impliquant des risques sanitaires non négligeables pour l’Homme et [SOURCE: Page_30]

les animaux. Récemment, de nombreux travaux se sont intéressés aux effets phytotoxiques [SOURCE: Page_30]

des cyanotoxines sur des plantes à vocation agricole. Dans cette revue bibliographique, nous [SOURCE: Page_30]

avons dans un premier temps, présenter les différentes cyanotoxines et leurs modes d’action [SOURCE: Page_30]

sur l’Homme et les animaux ainsi que la fréquence de ces toxines dans les plantes. Dans un [SOURCE: Page_30]

second temps, les concentrations environnementales en cyanotoxines dans les eaux douces [SOURCE: Page_30]

ainsi que leur devenir dans les milieux aquatiques et terrestres sont présentées. Finalement, [SOURCE: Page_30]

nous présenterons les résultats obtenus sur la bioaccumulation de cyanotoxines dans les [SOURCE: Page_30]

plantes utilisées comme source alimentaire et les conséquences sur la santé animale et [SOURCE: Page_30]

humaine. De façon générale, notre étude bibliographique montre que les informations [SOURCE: Page_30]

concernant les effets des cyanotoxines sur les organismes terrestres sont particulièrement rares [SOURCE: Page_30]

et qu’il y a un manque de connaissance sur le devenir de ces toxines dans les sols ainsi que sur [SOURCE: Page_30]

leurs effets phytotoxiques. [SOURCE: Page_30]

Mots-clefs : Cyanobactéries, Cyanotoxines, Microcystines, Anatoxines, Cylindrospermopsin, [SOURCE: Page_30]

Devenir [SOURCE: Page_30]

11 [SOURCE: Page_30]

Etude Bibliographique [SOURCE: Page_31]

Abstract: [SOURCE: Page_31]

The occurrence of harmful cyanobacterial blooms in surface waters is often accompanied [SOURCE: Page_31]

by the production of a variety of cyanotoxins. These toxins are designed to target in humans [SOURCE: Page_31]

and animals specific organs on which they act: hepatotoxins (liver), neurotoxins (nervous [SOURCE: Page_31]

system), cytotoxic alkaloids, and dermatotoxins (skin), but they often have important side [SOURCE: Page_31]

effects too. When introduced into the soil ecosystem by spray irrigation of crops they may [SOURCE: Page_31]

affect the same molecular pathways in plants having identical or similar target organs, tissues, [SOURCE: Page_31]

cells or biomolecules. There are also several indications that terrestrial plants, including food [SOURCE: Page_31]

crop plants, can bioaccumulate cyanotoxins and present, therefore, potential health hazards [SOURCE: Page_31]

for human and animals. The number of publications concerned with phytotoxic effects of [SOURCE: Page_31]

cyanotoxins on agricultural plants has increased recently. In this review, we first examine [SOURCE: Page_31]

different cyanotoxins and their modes of actions in humans and mammals and occurrence of [SOURCE: Page_31]

target biomolecules in vegetable organisms. Then we present environmental concentrations of [SOURCE: Page_31]

cyanotoxins in freshwaters and their fate in aquatic and soil ecosystems. Finally, we highlight [SOURCE: Page_31]

bioaccumulation of cyanotoxins in plants used for feed and food and its consequences on [SOURCE: Page_31]

animals and human health. Overall, our review shows that the information on the effects of [SOURCE: Page_31]

cyanotoxins on non-target organisms in the terrestrial environment is particularly scarce, and [SOURCE: Page_31]

that there are still serious gaps in the knowledge about the fate in the soil ecosystems and [SOURCE: Page_31]

phytotoxicity of these toxins. [SOURCE: Page_31]

Keywords: Cyanobacteria, Cyanotoxins, Microcystins, Anatoxins, Cylindrospermopsin, Fate [SOURCE: Page_31]

12 [SOURCE: Page_31]

Etude Bibliographique [SOURCE: Page_32]

1.1. Introduction In light of global climate change, and particularly measurable rises in global temperature, [SOURCE: Page_32]

as well as increased fluxes of certain nutrients (i.e., nitrates, phosphates) brought either by [SOURCE: Page_32]

agricultural run-off or by sewage treatment plants and other anthropogenic sources, it has [SOURCE: Page_32]

been suggested that cyanobacteria, including toxin-producing taxa, may be increasing in [SOURCE: Page_32]

abundance, and thus represent an emerging human and environmental health concern (For [SOURCE: Page_32]

review see in O’Neil et al., 2012). The presence of such toxins has been reported throughout [SOURCE: Page_32]

the world and it appears that livertoxic microcystins are more commonly found in 40–75% [SOURCE: Page_32]

cyanobacterial blooms (Sivonen and Jones, 1999). The contamination of surface waters by [SOURCE: Page_32]

these cyanotoxins can cause water quality problems for fisheries, aquaculture, farming, and [SOURCE: Page_32]

sanitary hazard for human and animals. Humans are exposed to cyanobacteria toxins through [SOURCE: Page_32]

many routes, including drinking water, recreational contact, and health food products made [SOURCE: Page_32]

from cyanobacteria, and food chain. In recent years, several cyanobacterial toxins were [SOURCE: Page_32]

investigated in regard to their ability to enter the food chain via freshwater seafood (Ibelings [SOURCE: Page_32]

and Chorus, 2007; Ettoumi et al., 2011), however, their ability to enter the food chain via [SOURCE: Page_32]

agricultural crops has not been thoroughly investigated to date. Although no case of poisoning [SOURCE: Page_32]

by these products has been reported in the literature, this eventuality must not be ignored. [SOURCE: Page_32]

Indeed, a recent epidemiological study showed that the excessive incidence of amyotrophic [SOURCE: Page_32]

lateral sclerosis in the population of the islands of Guam in the Pacific was linked to a [SOURCE: Page_32]

consumption of the seeds of cycas contaminated by a neurotoxin, b-methylamino-Lalanine [SOURCE: Page_32]

(BMAA), produced by a species of cyanobacteria of the genus Nostoc living in symbiosis in [SOURCE: Page_32]

the roots of this plant (Banack and Cox, 2003; Cox et al., 2003; Murch et al., 2004; Steele and [SOURCE: Page_32]

McGeer, 2008). This last cited fact is gaining importance since plants could in a direct or [SOURCE: Page_32]

indirect manner contribute to food chain cyanotoxin’s transfer, and by the way constitute a [SOURCE: Page_32]

potent health risk source. Indeed, numerous studies reported that both submerged and [SOURCE: Page_32]

13 [SOURCE: Page_32]

Etude Bibliographique [SOURCE: Page_33]

emergent aquatic plants have been shown to absorb microcystins from low external [SOURCE: Page_33]

concentrations (Pflugmacher et al., 1998; Yin et al., 2005; Saqrane et al., 2007). In terrestrial [SOURCE: Page_33]

plants, Codd et al. (1999) reported that spray irrigation of commercial lettuce (Lactuca sativa) [SOURCE: Page_33]

plants with water containing Microcystis resulted in colonies and single cells of the [SOURCE: Page_33]

cyanobacterium being lodged on the leaves 10 d (days) after the last irrigation. MC-LR was [SOURCE: Page_33]

present at 2.5 mg kg -1 dry weight (DW) in the central leaves, 0.833 mg kg -1 (DW) in the distal [SOURCE: Page_33]

zone of mature leaves, and 0.094 mg kg-1 (DW) in the basal zone of mature leaves. The last [SOURCE: Page_33]

study indicated that toxins were absorbed by the plant as the central leaves would have been [SOURCE: Page_33]

protected from irrigation. Similar conclusions were reached for rice (Oryza sativa) and rape [SOURCE: Page_33]

(Brassica napus) by Chen et al. (2004). Therefore, the accumulation of cyanotoxins in the [SOURCE: Page_33]

terrestrial food chain is at present remains more worrying and the proposed quality limits are [SOURCE: Page_33]

rare, indeed, many aspects concerning these toxins are particularly scarce, notably those [SOURCE: Page_33]

relative to the fate of cyanotoxins in the soil ecosystems and their toxicity and [SOURCE: Page_33]

bioaccumulation on agricultural crops. There have been several reviews of the intensification [SOURCE: Page_33]

and global expansion of harmful cyanoabcterial blooms in terms of abundance, geographic [SOURCE: Page_33]

extent, factors that may be promoting this expansion, and prevention and management of [SOURCE: Page_33]

cyanobacteiral blooms and their toxins, as well as effects on aquatic ecosystem health and [SOURCE: Page_33]

transfer on food webs (Wiegand and Pflugmacher, 2005; Ibelings and Chorus, 2007; Paerl and [SOURCE: Page_33]

Huisman, 2009; Aráoz et al., 2010; Kinnear, 2010; Merel et al., 2010; Jančula and Maršálek, [SOURCE: Page_33]

2011; O’Neil et al., 2012). However, the purpose of this review is to: (1) Highlight important [SOURCE: Page_33]

findings of the last decade of modes of actions of cyanotoxins in humans and mammals and [SOURCE: Page_33]

occurrence of target biomolecules in vegetable organisms; (2) Describe the fate of [SOURCE: Page_33]

cyanotoxins in aquatic and soil ecosystems and focus in their phytotoxicity; and (3) [SOURCE: Page_33]

Emphasize bioaccumulation of these toxins in vegetable foods and its consequences on [SOURCE: Page_33]

animals and human health. [SOURCE: Page_33]

14 [SOURCE: Page_33]

Etude Bibliographique [SOURCE: Page_34]

1.2. Cyanotoxins and their producers Recent research suggests that eutrophication and climate change are two processes they [SOURCE: Page_34]

may promote the proliferation and expansion of harmful cyanobacterial blooms in freshwater, [SOURCE: Page_34]

estuarine, and marine ecosystems. These microorganisms are known to biosynthesize a wide [SOURCE: Page_34]

range of chemical classes of secondary metabolites such as peptides, macrolides, and [SOURCE: Page_34]

glycosides (Patterson et al., 1994; Namikoshi and Rinehart, 1996) possessing a number of [SOURCE: Page_34]

bioactivities: antiviral (Patterson et al. 1993, 1994), antifungal (Patterson et al., 1994), [SOURCE: Page_34]

cytotoxic (Patterson et al., 1991), enzymatic inhibitor (Honkanen et al., 1995), antineoplastic [SOURCE: Page_34]

(Moore, 1996), and allelopathic (Pushparaj et al., 1998). However, some of these [SOURCE: Page_34]

cyanobacterial secondary metabolites encompass a diversity of alkaloid and peptide [SOURCE: Page_34]

cyanotoxins which have been suggested to both pose threats to human and environmental [SOURCE: Page_34]

health worldwide (Hawkins et al., 1985; Carmichael and Falconer, 1993; Kuiper-Goodman et [SOURCE: Page_34]

al., 1999; Sivonen and Jones, 1999; Hitzfeld et al., 2000; Ettoumi et al., 2011). Toxic [SOURCE: Page_34]

cyanobacteria that have been involved in such incidents belong essentially to the genera [SOURCE: Page_34]

Microcystis, Anabaena, Aphanizomenon, Planktothrix, Oscillatoria, Cylindrospermopsis and [SOURCE: Page_34]

less often Gomphosphaeria, Coelosphaerium, Gloeotrichia, Nodularia and Nostoc (Hawkins [SOURCE: Page_34]

et al., 1985; Sivonen and Jones, 1999). The cyanotoxins are essentially endotoxins which can [SOURCE: Page_34]

be released in the environment following a cellular lyse (Codd et al., 1989) or following [SOURCE: Page_34]

treatment of cyanobacterial blooms with algaecides (Kenefick et al., 1993). They can be [SOURCE: Page_34]

classified into four families according to the organs on which they act: neurotoxins (nervous [SOURCE: Page_34]

system), hepatotoxins (liver), cytotoxins (several organs: liver, kidneys, adrenal glands, small [SOURCE: Page_34]

intestine), and dermatotoxins (irritant toxins). Cyanobacterial neurotoxins (Figure 3) are [SOURCE: Page_34]

divided in three groups: anatoxins (anatoxin-a, homoanatoxin-a, and anatoxin-a(s)), [SOURCE: Page_34]

saxitoxins, and the neurotoxic amino acid L-beta-N-methylamino-L-alanine (BMAA). [SOURCE: Page_34]

Anatoxins and the BMAA are specific of cyanobacteria, while, saxitoxins are also synthesized [SOURCE: Page_34]

15 [SOURCE: Page_34]

Etude Bibliographique [SOURCE: Page_35]

by some marine dinoflagellates and associated with the human disease paralytic shellfish [SOURCE: Page_35]

poisoning or PSP (Falconer, 1991; Carmichael, 1994; Kaebernick and Neilan, 2001). By [SOURCE: Page_35]

contrast to the other neurotoxins which production depends on the phylogeny of the species, [SOURCE: Page_35]

the BMAA can be produced by almost all groups of cyanobacteria from freshwater, brackish, [SOURCE: Page_35]

and marine environments (Cox et al., 2005; Banack et al., 2007). [SOURCE: Page_35]

Figure 3: Structures of several neurotoxins, (A) anatoxin-a, (B) homoanatoxin, (C) anatoxin-a(s), (D) saxitoxin and (E) amino acid L-beta-N-methylamino-L-alanine (BMAA). [SOURCE: Page_35]

Hepatotoxins (Figure 4) are divided into two groups: Microcystins (MCs), cyclic [SOURCE: Page_35]

heptapeptide hepatotoxins (MW 900–1200), that are regarded as the most frequently [SOURCE: Page_35]

occurring and widespread of the cyanotoxins with more than 80 MC variants already reported [SOURCE: Page_35]

(Sivonen and Jones, 1999; Cox et al., 2005; del Campo and Ouahid, 2010); and nodularins [SOURCE: Page_35]

16 [SOURCE: Page_35]

Etude Bibliographique [SOURCE: Page_36]

(NODs) (MW 800–900) composed of five amino acids with only nine different natural [SOURCE: Page_36]

analogs have been characterized (de Silva et al., 1992; Namikoshi et al., 1993; Rinehart et al., [SOURCE: Page_36]

1994; Codd et al., 2005). The hydrophilic alkaloid cytotoxin, cylindrospermopsin (MW 415) [SOURCE: Page_36]

has been identified in the freshwater cyanobacteria Cylindrospermopsis raciborskii (Ohtani et [SOURCE: Page_36]

al., 1992), Umezakia natans (Harada et al., 2004), Aphanizomenon ovalisporum (Sivonen and [SOURCE: Page_36]

Jones, 1999), Anabaena sp. (Schembri et al., 2001), and Raphidiopsis sp. (Li et al., 2001). [SOURCE: Page_36]

Today,   only    two    congeners    of   cylindrospermopsin     have   been    identified:   7- [SOURCE: Page_36]

epicylindrospermopsin and deoxycylindrospermopsin. The freshwater cyanobacterial irritant [SOURCE: Page_36]

toxins such as lipopolysaccharides, or endotoxins as they are commonly called, are major [SOURCE: Page_36]

components of the cell wall in most Gram-negative bacteria including cyanobacteria (Jann [SOURCE: Page_36]

and Jann, 1984; Mayer and Weckesser, 1984; Kaya, 1996; Stewart et al., 2006). [SOURCE: Page_36]

### 1.3.1. Neurotoxins

Anatoxin-a is a potent postsynaptic depolarizing neuromuscular blocking agent that [SOURCE: Page_38]

affects both nicotinic and muscarinic acetyl cholineacetylcholine receptors (Carmichael et al., [SOURCE: Page_38]

1979; Spivak et al., 1980). It acts as a depolarizing neuromuscular blocking agent mimicking [SOURCE: Page_38]

the action of acetylcholine. However, this neurotoxin is not degraded by the [SOURCE: Page_38]

acetylcholinesterase, and consequently its action on the muscular cells does not stop and, due [SOURCE: Page_38]

to being stimulated, these cells are blocked and thereby resulting to muscle paralysis [SOURCE: Page_38]

(Carmichael, 1994; Lilleheil et al., 1997). When the respiratory muscles are affected, the [SOURCE: Page_38]

insufficient oxygenation of the brain engenders convulsions and the oppression (Carmichael, [SOURCE: Page_38]

1994; Humpage et al., 1994). The LD50 (lethal dose resulting in 50% deaths) of this [SOURCE: Page_38]

neurotoxin is 200 µg kg-1 (mouse, i.p.) (Carmichael et al., 1979; Skulberg et al., 1992). [SOURCE: Page_38]

Homoanatoxin-a is a homologue of anatoxin-a, that was reported to be a potent nicotinic [SOURCE: Page_38]

agonist (Wonnacott et al., 1992). It enhances the release of acetylcholine from peripheral [SOURCE: Page_38]

cholinergic nerves through opening of endogenous voltage dependent neuronal L-type [SOURCE: Page_38]

calcium channels (Aas et al., 1996; Lilleheil et al., 1997). Despite the similarity in their [SOURCE: Page_38]

names, anatoxin-a(s) and anatoxin-a are not structurally related (Figure 3) and exhibit [SOURCE: Page_38]

different physiological properties. Anatoxina(s) belongs to the organophosphate class of [SOURCE: Page_38]

neurotoxins and it acts as an irreversible inhibition of acetylcholinesterase at the nerve [SOURCE: Page_38]

synapse (Mahmood and Carmichael, 1986, 1987). The LD50 (mouse, i.p.) of this toxin is [SOURCE: Page_38]

about 20–40 µg kg-1 (Mahmood and Carmichael, 1987; Matsunaga et al., 1989; Carmichael et [SOURCE: Page_38]

al., 1990). In animals, the mechanisms of action of paralytic shellfish poisoning toxins [SOURCE: Page_38]

(saxitoxins) are based on the blockage of sodium conductance in axons (Kao et al., 1967; [SOURCE: Page_38]

Henderson et al., 1973). They so inhibit the transmission of the electric activity and prevent [SOURCE: Page_38]

the liberation of the acetylcholine (Nishiyama, 1968). Their toxicity is more important than [SOURCE: Page_38]

19 [SOURCE: Page_38]

Etude Bibliographique [SOURCE: Page_39]

that of anatoxins with a LD50 (mouse, i.p.) in the same conditions for the saxitoxin of 10 µg [SOURCE: Page_39]

kg-1 (Gorham and Carmichael, 1988). Saxitoxins can also bind to calcium (Ca+2) and [SOURCE: Page_39]

potassium (K+) channels, interfering with the speed of opening and closing of these channels, [SOURCE: Page_39]

which can in turn lead to alteration in the influx of ions to the cell (Wang et al., 2003; Su et [SOURCE: Page_39]

al., 2004). In addition, the Na+-channel blockage may alter the selective permeability of the [SOURCE: Page_39]

membrane and may change the flow of ions, leading to damage to cellular homeostasis (Hille, [SOURCE: Page_39]

1992; Jablonski et al., 2007). Concerning the neurotoxic amino acid (BMAA), it acts in [SOURCE: Page_39]

mammals as a glutamate agonist at AMPA, kainite and NMDA receptors (Spencer et al., [SOURCE: Page_39]

1986, 1987; Andersson et al., 1997; Seawright et al., 1999). Consequently, it increases the [SOURCE: Page_39]

intracellular concentration of calcium in neurons and induces neuronal activity by [SOURCE: Page_39]

hyperexcitation (Brownson et al., 2002). To our knowledge, no data regarding the toxicity of [SOURCE: Page_39]

cyanobacterial neurotoxins in higher plants have been reported. However, interfering of some [SOURCE: Page_39]

of them such as saxitoxins with the speed of opening and closing of Na+,Ca$_2$+ and K+ channels [SOURCE: Page_39]

could modify ions transport in plant cells. For example, a modification of sodium signals can [SOURCE: Page_39]

modify osmotic pressure in cells or the assimilation of CO$_2$ for C$_4$ plants (Brownell and [SOURCE: Page_39]

Crossland, 1972). While sodium extrusion in animal cells and microorganisms (including [SOURCE: Page_39]

yeast) is directly energized by ATP hydrolysis (Na+-ATPases), these Na+ pumps are absent [SOURCE: Page_39]

from higher plants (Horie and Schroeder, 2004). [SOURCE: Page_39]

### 1.3.2. Hepatotoxins

Cyanobacterial hepatotoxins type microcystin-LR are generally not able to penetrate [SOURCE: Page_39]

vertebrate cell membranes and therefore, require uptake via the bile acid transport system [SOURCE: Page_39]

present in hepatocytes and cells lining the small intestine (Runnegar et al., 1991). As a result [SOURCE: Page_39]

of this, toxicity of these cyanotoxins is restricted to organs expressing the organic anion [SOURCE: Page_39]

transporter on their cell membranes such as the liver (Fischer et al., 2005). However, in [SOURCE: Page_39]

vegetable cells one relatively unexplored question regarding these toxins concerns the [SOURCE: Page_39]

20 [SOURCE: Page_39]

Etude Bibliographique [SOURCE: Page_40]

mechanism of uptake, particularly the variants that would be predicted to be membrane [SOURCE: Page_40]

impermeable based on polarity. They may cross cell membranes of plants by other [SOURCE: Page_40]

mechanisms, including diffusion or by root absorption. Pflugmacher et al. (2001) have been [SOURCE: Page_40]

reported that when the emergent reed plant P. australis was exposed to 0.5 µg of 14C-labeled [SOURCE: Page_40]

microcystin-LR L-1 for 3 d, it demonstrated a rapid uptake (since 0.5 h) of the toxin. The main [SOURCE: Page_40]

uptake route appeared to be in the stem and rhizome, from which the toxin is transported into [SOURCE: Page_40]

the higher parts of the plant to the leaves. Uptake directly through the leaves may also occur [SOURCE: Page_40]

by direct contact of small plants or by the lowest leaves of a plant with surface water and with [SOURCE: Page_40]

upper leaves by wave and spray contact (Pflugmacher et al., 2001). Once in both vertebrate [SOURCE: Page_40]

and vegetable cells, microcystins and nodularins have been shown to be potent and specific [SOURCE: Page_40]

inhibitors of protein phosphatases 1 and 2A, and this inhibition accounts for their extreme [SOURCE: Page_40]

toxicity (MacKintosh et al., 1990; Kurki-Helasmo and Meriluoto, 1998; Hastie et al., 2005). [SOURCE: Page_40]

Those proteins are involved in several physiological and molecular processes in higher plants [SOURCE: Page_40]

(Sheen, 1993; Takeda et al., 1994). Indeed, numerous studies reported that microcystins have [SOURCE: Page_40]

several perturbatory effects on plant physiology and metabolism, when sufficient levels of [SOURCE: Page_40]

toxin enter the plant cells (MacKintosh et al., 1990; Siegl et al., 1990; Sheen, 1993; [SOURCE: Page_40]

Yamasaki, 1993; Smith et al., 1994; Abe et al., 1996; Smith, 1996; Kurki-Helasmo and [SOURCE: Page_40]

Meriluoto, 1998; Weiss et al., 2000; McElhiney et al., 2001; Pflugmacher, 2002; [SOURCE: Page_40]

Romanowska-Duda and Tarczyńska, 2002; Gehringer et al., 2003; Chen et al., 2004, 2011; [SOURCE: Page_40]

Mitrovic et al., 2005; Saqrane et al., 2007, 2008; Stüven and Pflugmacher, 2007; Järvenpää et [SOURCE: Page_40]

al., 2007; Jang et al., 2007; Peuthert et al., 2008; Máthé et al., 2009; Huang et al., 2009; El [SOURCE: Page_40]

Khalloufi et al., 2011, 2012; Jámbrik et al., 2011; Perron et al., 2012). On the other hand, [SOURCE: Page_40]

several studies have also reported that these hepatotoxins induce oxidative stress in mammal [SOURCE: Page_40]

cells (Žegura et al., 2003; Botha et al., 2004; Boua cha and Maatouk, 2004; Puerto et al., [SOURCE: Page_40]

2010). Therefore, their toxicity on aquatic plants seems to be also more linked to the induction [SOURCE: Page_40]

21 [SOURCE: Page_40]

Etude Bibliographique [SOURCE: Page_41]

of oxidative stress manifested by elevated reactive oxygen species (ROS) production and [SOURCE: Page_41]

malondialdehyde content (Lefevre et al., 1950; Pflugmacher, 2004; Hu et al., 2005; Leflaive [SOURCE: Page_41]

and Ten-Hage, 2007). [SOURCE: Page_41]

### 1.3.3. Cytotoxins

The alkaloid cylindrospermopsin (CYN) is known as a general cytotoxin that blocks [SOURCE: Page_41]

protein synthesis in mammal cells (Runnegar et al., 1995; Froscio et al., 2001, 2003). [SOURCE: Page_41]

Implications of this effect can be also observed in vegetable cells. In fact, Froscio et al. (2008) [SOURCE: Page_41]

reported that CYN was shown to inhibit the eukaryotic protein synthesis apparatus with [SOURCE: Page_41]

similar potency in plant and mammalian cell extracts, half maximal inhibitory concentration [SOURCE: Page_41]

of 334 nM in wheat germ extract and 110 nM in reticulocyte lysate. Metcalf et al. (2004) also [SOURCE: Page_41]

showed that CYN inhibited pollen germination in tobacco plants (Nicotiana tabacum), with [SOURCE: Page_41]

partial inhibition of protein production in the germinating pollen tubes following exposure to [SOURCE: Page_41]

138 µg mL-1 of toxin. [SOURCE: Page_41]

1.4. Environmental concentrations of cyanotoxins in freshwaters and fate in aquatic and soil ecosystems [SOURCE: Page_41]

### 1.4.1. Environmental concentrations of cyanotoxins

The occurrence of cyanobacterial toxins was reported throughout the world in surface [SOURCE: Page_41]

waters, where hepatotoxic microcystins are more commonly found in 50–75% cyanobacterial [SOURCE: Page_41]

blooms (Ettoumi et al., 2011). Data on environmental concentrations of cyanotoxins have [SOURCE: Page_41]

been compiled and reviewed in numerous papers (Sivonen and Jones, 1999; Falconer and [SOURCE: Page_41]

Humpage, 2006; van Apeldoorn et al., 2007; Messineo et al., 2009). In this review, we give a [SOURCE: Page_41]

summary on environmental concentrations focusing on irrigation waters with the ultimate aim [SOURCE: Page_41]

to relate them to phytotoxicological data. Cyanotoxins are intracellular toxins contained [SOURCE: Page_41]

within living cells, depending on both the nature of the toxin and the growth stage (Jungmann [SOURCE: Page_41]

et al., 1996; Orr and Jones, 1998; Park et al., 1998a,b; Sivonen and Jones, 1999). They are 22 [SOURCE: Page_41]

Etude Bibliographique [SOURCE: Page_42]

only released into the water, to form dissolved toxin, during cell senescence or cell death and [SOURCE: Page_42]

lysis or through water treatment processes such as algaecide application, rather than by [SOURCE: Page_42]

continuous excretion (James and Fawell, 1991; Gupta et al., 2001; Babica et al., 2006). The [SOURCE: Page_42]

highest total (intracellular plus dissolved) cyanotoxin levels have been found in blooms and [SOURCE: Page_42]

scums. For example, total MC concentrations in surface waters vary from trace to several [SOURCE: Page_42]

milligrams per liter, being strongly influenced by the occurrence of these forms of [SOURCE: Page_42]

cyanobacterial biomass. In surface waters used as irrigation source, total MC concentrations [SOURCE: Page_42]

of 4–50 µg L-1, up to 6,500 µg L-1, have been reported in multiple locations, including but not [SOURCE: Page_42]

limited to the Morocco (Oudra et al., 2001), Tunisia (El Herry et al., 2008), India (Prakash et [SOURCE: Page_42]

al., 2009), Turkey (Gurbuz et al., 2009), and Finland (Spoof et al., 2003), but much higher [SOURCE: Page_42]

levels up to 29,000 µg L-1 in Algeria (Nasri et al., 2008). It should be noted, however, that [SOURCE: Page_42]

these very high concentrations of cyanotoxins would be from scums or from very dense [SOURCE: Page_42]

cyanobacterial biomass. In the field, water samples with more than 1 µg L-1 total MCs, [SOURCE: Page_42]

dissolved fraction did not comprise more than 10% of the combined intra and extracellular [SOURCE: Page_42]

pool (Lindholm and Meriluoto, 1991; Jones and Orr, 1994; Tsuji et al., 1996; Ueno et al., [SOURCE: Page_42]

1996; Lahti et al., 1997). As well in some laboratory studies, where both intracellular and [SOURCE: Page_42]

extracellular cyclic peptide toxins and saxitoxins have been measured, it is generally the case [SOURCE: Page_42]

that in healthy log phase cultures, less than 10–20% of the total toxin pool is dissolved in the [SOURCE: Page_42]

culture medium (Sivonen et al., 1990; Lehtimaki et al., 1997; Negri et al., 1997; Rapala et al., [SOURCE: Page_42]

1997). On the contrary, CYN may often be found at higher levels in dissolved form than [SOURCE: Page_42]

within cells, as it readily leaks from cells under normal growth conditions (Norris et al., 2001; [SOURCE: Page_42]

Falconer and Humpage, 2006; Wormer et al., 2008). For example, Shaw et al. (1999) found [SOURCE: Page_42]

that in two instances of A. ovalisporum blooms around 80% of the total toxin content of the [SOURCE: Page_42]

water was in free solution. Recently, Messineo et al. (2009) reported that in several Italian [SOURCE: Page_42]

lakes of different characteristics and human uses, extracellular concentrations of total CYN [SOURCE: Page_42]

23 [SOURCE: Page_42]

Etude Bibliographique [SOURCE: Page_43]

varied from non-detectable values up to 126 µg L-1. However, limited or no information is [SOURCE: Page_43]

available about the proportion of dissolved form with respect to the total level for the [SOURCE: Page_43]

cyanobacterial neurotoxins. [SOURCE: Page_43]

Etude Bibliographique [SOURCE: Page_46]

Once they enter in aquatic and soil ecosystems, cyanotoxins can be removed according to [SOURCE: Page_46]

various processes such as photochemical degradation by UV, adsorption in particles in [SOURCE: Page_46]

suspension or onto sediments, and biodegradation (Tsuji et al., 1994; Rapala et al., 1994; [SOURCE: Page_46]

Lahti et al., 1996; Chiswell et al., 1999; Welker and Steinberg, 1999; Kaebernick and Neilan, [SOURCE: Page_46]

2001; Mazur-Marzec et al., 2006; Wormer et al., 2008; Burns et al., 2009; Klitzke et al., 2010, [SOURCE: Page_46]

2011; Thirumavalavan et al., 2012). However, the four groups of cyanotoxins: hepatotoxins, [SOURCE: Page_46]

neurotoxins, cytotoxins, and dermatotoxins, exhibit quite different chemical stabilities in these [SOURCE: Page_46]

ecosystems. Hepatotoxin cyclic peptide cyanotoxins, microcystins and nodularins, are [SOURCE: Page_46]

extremely stable compounds and may persist in aquatic systems for weeks after being released [SOURCE: Page_46]

from the cells (Jones and Orr, 1994; Chen et al., 2008; Edwards et al., 2008). According to [SOURCE: Page_46]

other studies, these toxins in natural conditions could persist for several months or years [SOURCE: Page_46]

(Harada et al., 1996; Sivonen and Jones, 1999). However, numerous studies reported that [SOURCE: Page_46]

photochemical degradation by sunlight UV and exposure to degrading bacteria may speed up [SOURCE: Page_46]

their removal from the water (Bourne et al., 1996; Heresztyn and Nicholson, 1997; Sivonen [SOURCE: Page_46]

and Jones, 1999; Park et al., 2001; Song et al., 2009; Ho et al., 2012). The photodegradation [SOURCE: Page_46]

of MCs in full sunlight can take as little as two weeks or longer than six weeks, depending on [SOURCE: Page_46]

the presence of water-soluble cell pigments (Tsuji et al., 1994; Welker and Steinberg, 2000). [SOURCE: Page_46]

More recently, Thirumavalavan et al. (2012) showed in a laboratory experiment that the [SOURCE: Page_46]

presence of humic acid and turbidity affected the photo-degradation process. Additionally, in [SOURCE: Page_46]

sea water the rate of nodularin photolysis can be accelerated by the presence of some cell [SOURCE: Page_46]

components and humic substance (Welker and Steinberg, 1999). Conversely, during the [SOURCE: Page_46]

benthic phase, the photodegradation of these cyanotoxins is expected to be almost negligible [SOURCE: Page_46]

due to low radiation penetration (    rmer et al., 2010). In fact, Welker and Steinberg (2000) [SOURCE: Page_46]

found that the half-life of MCs in the deep lakes is longer than the season of cyanobacteria [SOURCE: Page_46]

growth, what suggests that the photolysis is significant only for shallow lakes. The alkaloid [SOURCE: Page_46]

27 [SOURCE: Page_46]

Etude Bibliographique [SOURCE: Page_47]

cytotoxin, CYN, is relatively stable in the dark; however, in sunlight and in the presence of [SOURCE: Page_47]

cell pigments degradation occurs quite rapidly with more than 90% within 2–3 d (Chiswell et [SOURCE: Page_47]

al., 1999). The neurotoxin, anatoxin-a, is also relatively stable in the dark, but it undergoes [SOURCE: Page_47]

rapid photochemical degradation in sunlight particularly in alkaline conditions, even in the [SOURCE: Page_47]

absence of cell pigments (Stevens and Krieger, 1991; Smith and Sutton, 1993). However, no [SOURCE: Page_47]

data   are   available   for   other   cyanobacterial   neurotoxins   and   lipopolysaccharides [SOURCE: Page_47]

dermatotoxins. Cyanotoxins can also be retained on suspended particles or onto sediments in [SOURCE: Page_47]

aquatic systems. Wörmer et al. (2011) showed the great importance of sedimentation [SOURCE: Page_47]

processes in the fate of MCs in freshwaters with an amount of toxin associated to settling [SOURCE: Page_47]

particles to be in the range of mg d -1 m-2. But other studies reported that no more than 20% of [SOURCE: Page_47]

toxins can be adsorbed on sediments (Rapala et al., 1993; Lahti et al., 1996). Furthermore, it [SOURCE: Page_47]

was suggested that the removal of cyanotoxins in this process was the result of both [SOURCE: Page_47]

adsorption and biodegradation (Lahti et al., 1996). Therefore, biodegradation would appear to [SOURCE: Page_47]

be the main fate for most cyanotoxins in aquatic systems and the relative performance of this [SOURCE: Page_47]

process would be very site specific and dependent upon local sediment characteristics and [SOURCE: Page_47]

microbial activity. It was recently reported that the data generated in laboratory and field [SOURCE: Page_47]

studies strongly indicate that, in shallow lakes, low persistence and natural eliminations of [SOURCE: Page_47]

MCs are due to biodegradation; suggesting that sediments play a crucial role in [SOURCE: Page_47]

biodegradation by continuously supplying toxin-degrading bacteria to the water column [SOURCE: Page_47]

(Chen et al., 2008; Mazur-Marzec et al., 2009; Chen et al., 2010). However, in deep [SOURCE: Page_47]

sediments, biodegradation might be limited due to anoxic conditions (Grützmacher et al., [SOURCE: Page_47]

2002; Holst et al., 2003; Gr tzmacher et al., 2009) and sediments only bring nutrients for [SOURCE: Page_47]

bacteria responsible of cyanotoxins biodegradation. Degradative heterotrophic bacteria of [SOURCE: Page_47]

hepatotoxic cyanotoxins (MCs and NOD), and cytotoxins (CYN) have been found in various [SOURCE: Page_47]

media, such as water columns (Jones and Orr, 1994; Cousins et al., 1996; Christoffersen et al., [SOURCE: Page_47]

28 [SOURCE: Page_47]

Etude Bibliographique [SOURCE: Page_48]

2002; Hyenstrand et al., 2003; Lemes et al., 2008; Mazur-Marzec et al., 2009; Chen et al., [SOURCE: Page_48]

2010), sediments (Rapala et al., 1994; Holst et al., 2003), sewage effluents (Lam et al., 1995) [SOURCE: Page_48]

or soils (Miller et al., 2001; Grützmacher et al., 2002), with specific enzymatic pathways well [SOURCE: Page_48]

characterized (Bourne et al., 1996; Okano et al., 2009; Zhang et al., 2010). Several previous [SOURCE: Page_48]

studies have been indicated that MCs can be degraded by aquatic bacteria identified as [SOURCE: Page_48]

pertaining especially to the genus Sphingomonas (Bourne et al., 1996; Harada et al., 2004; [SOURCE: Page_48]

Ishii et al., 2004; Maruyama et al., 2006; Manage et al., 2009). Therefore, a microcystin- [SOURCE: Page_48]

degrading gene cluster, mlrA, B, C and D was identified in these microorganisms, sequenced [SOURCE: Page_48]

and the degradation process was proposed (Bourne et al., 2001; Saito et al., 2003; Imanishi et [SOURCE: Page_48]

al., 2005). In the last two decades, several other species of bacteria capable of degrading [SOURCE: Page_48]

peptidic cyanotoxins were identified, Sphingomonas sp. strain ACM-3962 (Jones and Orr, [SOURCE: Page_48]

1994), Paucibacter toxinivorans (Rapala et al., 2005), Sphingosinicella microcystinivorans [SOURCE: Page_48]

(Maruyama et al., 2006), Burkholderia sp. (Lemes et al., 2008). The most toxic congener, [SOURCE: Page_48]

Microcystin-LR, was also found susceptible to breakdown by Sphingomonas, which initiated [SOURCE: Page_48]

ring-opening and the production of a linear compound 200 times less toxic (Bourne et al., [SOURCE: Page_48]

1996). Recently, Ho et al. (2012) identified another bacterium strain (TT25) whose genome is [SOURCE: Page_48]

similar to Sphingopyxis sp. that it is able to degrade MCs. The ability of these all species to [SOURCE: Page_48]

degrade other congeners of MCs and NODs was investigated and revealed that peptides with [SOURCE: Page_48]

the Adda–Arginine bond were successfully degraded while MC-LF, with Adda–Phenyalanine [SOURCE: Page_48]

bond and 6(z)-Adda-MC-LR and 6(z)-Adda-MC-RR were not significantly degraded [SOURCE: Page_48]

(Imanishi et al., 2005). Another Japanese Sphingomonas isolate, 7CY, was shown to degrade [SOURCE: Page_48]

a wider range of MCs, including MC-LR, -RR, -LY, -LW, and -LF but it was unable to [SOURCE: Page_48]

degrade NOD-Har a NOD analog where arginine is replaced by homoarginine (Ishii et al., [SOURCE: Page_48]

2004). Biodegradation has also been shown to be an important process for the removal of the [SOURCE: Page_48]

alkaloid cytotoxin, CYN, from contaminated water (Chiswell et al., 1999; Senogles et al., [SOURCE: Page_48]

29 [SOURCE: Page_48]

Etude Bibliographique [SOURCE: Page_49]

2002). By contrast, a laboratory study investigating biodegradation of CYN with bacterial [SOURCE: Page_49]

communities from two water bodies in Spain, one having frequent exposure to CYN, the other [SOURCE: Page_49]

rarely, has been shown that biodegradation of this toxin by an active microbial community [SOURCE: Page_49]

does not take place during a 40-d (Wormer et al., 2008). A recent study demonstrated that [SOURCE: Page_49]

CYN was degraded by indigenous microbial flora in waters with a history of [SOURCE: Page_49]

Cylindrospermopsis blooms (Smith et al., 2008). Despite isolation of many bacteria from [SOURCE: Page_49]

CYN enriched cultures, only a single isolate (Delftia sp.) capable of degrading CYN has been [SOURCE: Page_49]

obtained (Smith, 2005). However, for cyanobacterial neurotoxins there are few reports on [SOURCE: Page_49]

their persistence and biodegradation compared to cyanobacterial hepatotoxins, although the [SOURCE: Page_49]

increasing occurrence of these toxins in surface waters. A recent study indicated that [SOURCE: Page_49]

saxitoxins are predisposed to bacterial degradation during passage through bioactive treatment [SOURCE: Page_49]

plant (Kayal et al., 2008). However, this study showed that structural modification during the [SOURCE: Page_49]

biological treatment resulted to decrease of the predominant saxitoxin gonyautoxins variants [SOURCE: Page_49]

and an increase in variants of gonyautoxins 2 and 3 which are more toxic than the C-toxins. [SOURCE: Page_49]

Early work by Kiviranta et al. (1991) reported the isolation of a Pseudomonas sp. capable of [SOURCE: Page_49]

rapid degradation of anatoxin- a, with a rate of 6–30 mg mL-1 per 3 d. A later study reported [SOURCE: Page_49]

by Rapala et al. (1994) has been shown the removal of anatoxin-a by microbial populations [SOURCE: Page_49]

isolated from water and sediments of a eutrophic, oligotrophic, and humic lake. In conclusion, [SOURCE: Page_49]

the period of photodegradation of cyanotoxins is relatively long in comparison to the [SOURCE: Page_49]

degradation caused by the microbial activity. Recently, Hu et al. (2012) found that the [SOURCE: Page_49]

Bacillus sp. strain EMB is able to completely remove 2.99 mg L-1 of MC-RR and 2.15 mg L-1 [SOURCE: Page_49]

of MC-LR within 24 h. However, the biodegradation speed of cyanotoxins in aquatic [SOURCE: Page_49]

ecosystems can be influenced by the initial concentration and nature of toxins (Edwards et al., [SOURCE: Page_49]

2008; Ho et al., 2012) and by additional factors such as the water temperature (Park et al., [SOURCE: Page_49]

2001; Ho et al., 2007; Smith et al., 2008; Hoefel et al., 2009) and the bacterial community [SOURCE: Page_49]

30 [SOURCE: Page_49]

Etude Bibliographique [SOURCE: Page_50]

composition within the water body; not only the types of organisms present, but also their [SOURCE: Page_50]

abundance (Hoefel et al., 2009; Ho et al., 2012). Hoefel et al. (2009) have demonstrated a [SOURCE: Page_50]

direct relationship between the abundance of degrading organisms and the rate of degradation [SOURCE: Page_50]

of MC-LR. Furthermore, although MCs are degraded by most of bacteria species, it seems [SOURCE: Page_50]

that a lasting day’s delay or weeks are necessary before the degradation is introduced. This [SOURCE: Page_50]

result was in agreement with the conclusion of Hyenstrand et al. (2003) indicating that [SOURCE: Page_50]

bacteria species have to adapt themselves at first to the cyanobacteria metabolites before the [SOURCE: Page_50]

degradation of MCs becomes effective. Indeed, the results of this last study indicate a weaker [SOURCE: Page_50]

degradation of the MC-LR in May compared with September where the occurrence of [SOURCE: Page_50]

cyanobacteria is higher. Similarly, Smith et al. (2008) found that CYN was degraded by [SOURCE: Page_50]

indigenous microbial flora in waters with a history of Cylindrospermopsis blooms. The [SOURCE: Page_50]

physicochemical fate and the environmental concentrations of cyanotoxins in soil have been [SOURCE: Page_50]

the subject of a range of recent studies. Several classes of these toxins have been detected in [SOURCE: Page_50]

field soils, and the sorption behavior and degradation and transfer to vegetables have been [SOURCE: Page_50]

studied to a large extent (Morris et al., 2000; Miller et al., 2001; Chen et al., 2006b; Bibo et [SOURCE: Page_50]

al., 2008; Sathishkumar et al., 2011). The use of water from sources containing cyanobacterial [SOURCE: Page_50]

blooms and toxins for spray irrigation of terrestrial plants, including food crop plants presents [SOURCE: Page_50]

both a harmful effect on growth and development of plants and on soil ecosystems and [SOURCE: Page_50]

potential health hazards through several exposure routes, including uptake into the food chain [SOURCE: Page_50]

and accumulation of toxins on the external surfaces of edible plant material. Questions, [SOURCE: Page_50]

therefore, arise about the persistence of total cyanotoxins (dissolved and within the [SOURCE: Page_50]

cyanobacterial cells) when reach the soil ecosystem to produce phytotoxic effects. Once reach [SOURCE: Page_50]

the soil ecosystem, cyanotoxins persist in the environment, depending on the efficiency of [SOURCE: Page_50]

degradation (i.e., photolysis, hydrolysis and bacterial degradation). Microcystins can persist in [SOURCE: Page_50]

agriculture soils for relatively long times, with a half-life ranging between 6 and 17.8 d (Chen [SOURCE: Page_50]

31 [SOURCE: Page_50]

Etude Bibliographique [SOURCE: Page_51]

et al., 2006b). Jones et al. (1995) reported that scums of M. aeruginosa that dry on the shores [SOURCE: Page_51]

of lakes may contain high concentrations of MCs for several months. Recently, Metcalf et al. [SOURCE: Page_51]

(2012) found that MCs were detected in herbarium specimens of cyanobacteria which had [SOURCE: Page_51]

been collected from aquatic and terrestrial environments in 11 countries throughout the world, [SOURCE: Page_51]

dried, and stored at ambient temperatures in the dark for up to 170 years. Microcystins were [SOURCE: Page_51]

also detected by HPLC and ELISA assays in desert crust samples from Qatar at [SOURCE: Page_51]

concentrations between 1.5 and 53.7 ng g -1 dry weight (Metcalf et al., 2012). Thus, the [SOURCE: Page_51]

persistence of these toxins within dried cyanobacterial cells for long period suggests that they [SOURCE: Page_51]

will be released back into the soil when re-immersed by irrigation water, particularly when [SOURCE: Page_51]

cyanobacterial blooms are used in some countries as an organic fertilizer Chen et al., [SOURCE: Page_51]

(2006a,b). However, as mentioned above for aquatic ecosystems, adsorption on sediments and [SOURCE: Page_51]

specially exposure to degrading bacteria may also speed up their removal from the soil. The [SOURCE: Page_51]

information on the adsorption of cyanotoxins in agriculture soil ecosystems is particularly [SOURCE: Page_51]

scarce. However, adsorption of cyanobacterial hepatotoxins was measured in several batch [SOURCE: Page_51]

studies to determine the applicability of bank filtration as an efficient removal strategy of [SOURCE: Page_51]

these toxins from drinking water. For example, in batch experiments Miller et al. (2001) [SOURCE: Page_51]

studied the adsorption of cyanobacterial hepatotoxins, MC-LR and NOD, in five soils with [SOURCE: Page_51]

different physicochemical properties collected from regions around South Australia. They [SOURCE: Page_51]

found that the soils with the high clay and/or organic carbon contents had the higher toxins [SOURCE: Page_51]

adsorption coefficients. In similar experiments, Miller and Fallowfield (2001) found that the [SOURCE: Page_51]

soils with the highest organic carbon content (2.9%) and the highest clay content (16.1%) [SOURCE: Page_51]

were the most effective at removing these toxins in batch experiments. However, the sandy [SOURCE: Page_51]

soil (98.5% sand) was incapable of the removal of cyanotoxins. This finding was supported [SOURCE: Page_51]

by Morris et al. (2000) who reported that the clay content and its quality may be more [SOURCE: Page_51]

important for the adsorption than other soil characteristics. However, Eynard et al. (2000) [SOURCE: Page_51]

32 [SOURCE: Page_51]

Etude Bibliographique [SOURCE: Page_52]

suggested that soil was unable to protect groundwater from cyanotoxins that originated from [SOURCE: Page_52]

surface waters. Thus, it seems that cyanotoxins sorption in soils is low and could potentially [SOURCE: Page_52]

result in their high bioavailability to soil organisms and plants. In several studies, it seems that [SOURCE: Page_52]

the major dissipation process for cyanotoxins in soil ecosystems is mainly via microbial [SOURCE: Page_52]

degradation (Miller and Fallowfield, 2001; Chen et al., 2006b). In fact, numerous soil bacteria [SOURCE: Page_52]

as Arthrobacter sp., Brevibacterium sp. and Rhodococcus sp. are able to breakdown MCs [SOURCE: Page_52]

(Manage et al., 2009). Bourne et al. (2001) observed the same thing with Sphingomonas sp. [SOURCE: Page_52]

that possesses a gene cluster involved in the degradation of MC-LR. Furthermore, Falconer et [SOURCE: Page_52]

al. (1983) and Lambert et al. (1996) conclude that sand filtration alone is unable to remove [SOURCE: Page_52]

dissolved cyanotoxins. However, slow sand filters can be expected to remove 99% of [SOURCE: Page_52]

dissolved cyanotoxins (Keijola et al., 1988; Grützmacher et al., 2002). This can be explained [SOURCE: Page_52]

by the formation of a biofilm on top of the filter that it allows for some biodegradation of [SOURCE: Page_52]

cyanotoxins in slow sand filtration. No data are available for other cyanoabcterial toxins such [SOURCE: Page_52]

as neurotoxins and dermatotoxins, but some degradation may be expected, again depending [SOURCE: Page_52]

on the chemical conditions of soil. In conclusion, the scarce results on the fate of cyanotoxins [SOURCE: Page_52]

in soil ecosystems are very variable, which do not allow affirming with certainty the [SOURCE: Page_52]

necessary time for a complete disappearance of these toxins. This variability ensues partially [SOURCE: Page_52]

from used methods (e.g. studies led in laboratory with non environmental concentrations of [SOURCE: Page_52]

toxins and in free-soil systems). Therefore, the fate of cyanotoxins in soil ecosystems will [SOURCE: Page_52]

require more studies before we are capable to formulate an opinion on their persistence and [SOURCE: Page_52]

uptake into the food chain. [SOURCE: Page_52]

33 [SOURCE: Page_52]

Etude Bibliographique [SOURCE: Page_53]

1.5. Phytotoxicity effects of cyanotoxins The information on the effects of cyanotoxins on non-target organisms in the terrestrial [SOURCE: Page_53]

environment is particularly scarce. However, despite the impressive amount of information on [SOURCE: Page_53]

their toxicity on mammals compiled during the last two decades, there are still serious gaps in [SOURCE: Page_53]

the knowledge about the phytotoxicity of these toxins. The phytotoxic effects of cyanotoxins [SOURCE: Page_53]

on higher plants were firstly focused on aquatic photoautotrophic organisms (algae and [SOURCE: Page_53]

macrophytes) that are naturally exposed to cyanotoxins (Harper, 1992; Papke et al., 1997; [SOURCE: Page_53]

Weiss et al., 2000; Yu et al., 2000; Ikawa et al., 2001; Pietsch et al., 2001; Mitrovic et al., [SOURCE: Page_53]

2004; Ha and Pflugmacher, 2013). Since few years, scientists were also interested by the [SOURCE: Page_53]

effect of these toxins on terrestrial plants because, irrigation waters from sources containing [SOURCE: Page_53]

cyanobacterial blooms and toxins are generally used without treatment for spraying [SOURCE: Page_53]

agricultural crops and plants that might, therefore, induce a food chain contamination with a [SOURCE: Page_53]

considerable health risk and potential economic losses. [SOURCE: Page_53]

### 1.5.1. Neurotoxins

The cyanobacterial neurotoxins have not received more research attention than have [SOURCE: Page_53]

cyanobacterial hepatotoxins. This is a consequence of the many livestock deaths caused by [SOURCE: Page_53]

cyanobacterial species producing hepatotoxic microcystins and their more widespread [SOURCE: Page_53]

occurrence rather than species producing neurotoxins (Ettoumi et al., 2011). In addition, the [SOURCE: Page_53]

recent inclusion of microcystin-LR as a toxic chemical in the World Health Organisation [SOURCE: Page_53]

drinking water guidelines has further accelerated investigation of the toxic effects of [SOURCE: Page_53]

microcystins on mammals and vegetables rather than cyanobacterial neurotoxins. Therefore, [SOURCE: Page_53]

there are only few studies reported in the literature on the effects of cyanoabcterial [SOURCE: Page_53]

neurotoxins on crops and plants. Mitrovic et al. (2004) were exposed the free-floating aquatic [SOURCE: Page_53]

plant L. minor and the filamentous macroalga Chladophora fracta to anatoxin-a at 0.1–25 µg [SOURCE: Page_53]

L-1 under laboratory conditions for 4–7 d. They found in both organisms significantly increase [SOURCE: Page_53]

34 [SOURCE: Page_53]

Etude Bibliographique [SOURCE: Page_54]

of peroxidase activity after 4 d exposure at 25 µg L-1 but not at lower concentrations. After [SOURCE: Page_54]

7 d exposure to this neurotoxin significant increase of glutathione-S-transferase activity and [SOURCE: Page_54]

reduction of photosynthetic oxygen production were observed at 5 and 20 µg L-1 but not at [SOURCE: Page_54]

lower concentrations in L. minor. In addition, Ha and Pflugmacher (2013) reported that this [SOURCE: Page_54]

alkaloid neurotoxin at an environmentally relevant concentration (15 µg L-1), induced [SOURCE: Page_54]

phytotoxic effects on the submerged aquatic macrophyte Ceratophyllum demersum, mediated [SOURCE: Page_54]

by oxidative stress. Recently, Esterhuizen-Londt et al. (2011) investigated in vitro study the [SOURCE: Page_54]

effect of BMAA at different environmentally concentrations (0.5, 1, 5, 50 and 100 µg L-1) for [SOURCE: Page_54]

24 h on the oxidative stress responses of the macrophyte C. demersum. The most pronounced [SOURCE: Page_54]

effects found were activity-inhibiting effects on all the oxidative stress response enzymes at [SOURCE: Page_54]

all exposure concentrations. However, enzymes not related to oxidative stress response were [SOURCE: Page_54]

not affected by the BMAA in these experiments. For other neurotoxins, the literature search [SOURCE: Page_54]

did not yield any results. [SOURCE: Page_54]
